People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. Little things pull me off. If someone’s talking in another room, or a car passes the window, my mind just goes there, and then I’ve lost what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. If I’m sitting in a meeting, or just watching television, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot… or rubbing my fingers together. Sometimes I fuss with whatever’s in my lap without really meaning to.\n\nDoctor: Are these symptoms causing you a lot of problems or distress in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it is. It’s started to cause strain at home, and at the community center too. My husband and some of the volunteers… they’ve started checking behind me because I don’t always keep the plans straight. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. Sometimes I fuss with whatever’s in my lap without really meaning to.\n\nDoctor: Are these symptoms causing you a lot of problems or distress in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it is. It’s started to cause strain at home, and at the community center too. My husband and some of the volunteers… they’ve started checking behind me because I don’t always keep the plans straight. It’s embarrassing, really.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these symptoms when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t remember all of it clearly, but in grade school my teachers used to tell my mother I was bright, just… scattered. Losing papers, talking when I shouldn’t, wiggling around before I’d finished my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. My husband says I cut in before he’s finished talking. And in group planning, I’ll do it too… mostly because I’m worried I’ll lose the thought if I don’t say it right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before questions have been completed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… maybe once in a while, but I can’t say I’ve really noticed that as a regular thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty waiting for your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. Waiting in line at the pharmacy, or waiting my turn to speak at a meeting… I get fidgety. Sometimes I’ll catch myself sighing, or I jump in sooner than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often intrude on others physically, such as cutting in line or interrupting others' activities?\n\nPatient: Not physically, no… I don’t think I cut in line or anything like that. It’s more that I interrupt — my husband says I cut him off before he’s done, or in group planning I’ll jump in because I’m afraid I’ll forget what I was going to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do things impulsively, like buying things on a whim or making sudden decisions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any big way. I might decide something small without thinking it through, but… I wouldn’t say it happens often.\n\nDoctor: Do you often engage in reckless or impulsive physical behaviors, such as reckless driving?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I’m pretty careful with things like driving. Nothing like that comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Were you significantly disorganized or forgetful as a child, such as often losing things or forgetting assignments?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Back in grade school, teachers would tell my mother I was bright, but kind of scattered. I lost papers a lot, and didn’t always finish my work before I was… fidgeting or talking when I shouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following through on instructions or finishing chores at home?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t have a lot of chores like that now… I suppose I finish what needs doing, most of the time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

If someone’s talking in another room, or a car passes the window, my mind just goes there, and then I’ve lost what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. If I’m sitting in a meeting, or just watching television, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot… or rubbing my fingers together. Sometimes I fuss with whatever’s in my lap without really meaning to.\n\nDoctor: Are these symptoms causing you a lot of problems or distress in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it is. It’s started to cause strain at home, and at the community center too. My husband and some of the volunteers… they’ve started checking behind me because I don’t always keep the plans straight. It’s embarrassing, really.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these symptoms when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t remember all of it clearly, but in grade school my teachers used to tell my mother I was bright, just… scattered. Losing papers, talking when I shouldn’t, wiggling around before I’d finished my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. My husband says I cut in before he’s finished talking. And in group planning, I’ll do it too… mostly because I’m worried I’ll lose the thought if I don’t say it right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before questions have been completed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… maybe once in a while, but I can’t say I’ve really noticed that as a regular thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty waiting for your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. Waiting in line at the pharmacy, or waiting my turn to speak at a meeting… I get fidgety. Sometimes I’ll catch myself sighing, or I jump in sooner than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often intrude on others physically, such as cutting in line or interrupting others' activities?\n\nPatient: Not physically, no… I don’t think I cut in line or anything like that. It’s more that I interrupt — my husband says I cut him off before he’s done, or in group planning I’ll jump in because I’m afraid I’ll forget what I was going to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do things impulsively, like buying things on a whim or making sudden decisions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any big way. I might decide something small without thinking it through, but… I wouldn’t say it happens often.\n\nDoctor: Do you often engage in reckless or impulsive physical behaviors, such as reckless driving?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I’m pretty careful with things like driving. Nothing like that comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Were you significantly disorganized or forgetful as a child, such as often losing things or forgetting assignments?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Back in grade school, teachers would tell my mother I was bright, but kind of scattered. I lost papers a lot, and didn’t always finish my work before I was… fidgeting or talking when I shouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following through on instructions or finishing chores at home?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t have a lot of chores like that now… I suppose I finish what needs doing, most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following through on tasks or activities in your leisure time?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. Like if I’m at a church planning meeting, or trying to read the bulletin, I’ll start off fine… and then after a bit I realize I missed a good part of what was said or written.\n\nDoctor: Did you exhibit excessive running, climbing, or physical restlessness as a child?\n\nPatient: Not so much running or climbing that I remember. More… restless, I guess. If I had to sit still too long, I’d get this feeling inside like I needed to get up, straighten something, or go check on something.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you lose track of time or forget about everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really. I might get interested in something for a while, but I can’t say I lose track of everything around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these symptoms at work or in other social situations beyond home and the community center?\n\nPatient: Well, I’m not working now, so I can’t really say about work. Socially… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed much beyond the usual places I go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose I’ve just been feeling like I’m always up and moving. Even on quiet days, I’m bouncing between things—the garden club list, my knitting bag, travel papers—and I don’t really finish one before I’m on to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble completing tasks because you lose focus or get distracted?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Little things pull me off. If someone’s talking in another room, or a car passes the window, my mind just goes there, and then I’ve lost what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. If I’m sitting in a meeting, or just watching television, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot… or rubbing my fingers together. Sometimes I fuss with whatever’s in my lap without really meaning to.\n\nDoctor: Are these symptoms causing you a lot of problems or distress in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it is. It’s started to cause strain at home, and at the community center too. My husband and some of the volunteers… they’ve started checking behind me because I don’t always keep the plans straight. It’s embarrassing, really.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these symptoms when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t remember all of it clearly, but in grade school my teachers used to tell my mother I was bright, just… scattered. Losing papers, talking when I shouldn’t, wiggling around before I’d finished my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. My husband says I cut in before he’s finished talking. And in group planning, I’ll do it too… mostly because I’m worried I’ll lose the thought if I don’t say it right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before questions have been completed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… maybe once in a while, but I can’t say I’ve really noticed that as a regular thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty waiting for your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. Waiting in line at the pharmacy, or waiting my turn to speak at a meeting… I get fidgety. Sometimes I’ll catch myself sighing, or I jump in sooner than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often intrude on others physically, such as cutting in line or interrupting others' activities?\n\nPatient: Not physically, no… I don’t think I cut in line or anything like that. It’s more that I interrupt — my husband says I cut him off before he’s done, or in group planning I’ll jump in because I’m afraid I’ll forget what I was going to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do things impulsively, like buying things on a whim or making sudden decisions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any big way. I might decide something small without thinking it through, but… I wouldn’t say it happens often.\n\nDoctor: Do you often engage in reckless or impulsive physical behaviors, such as reckless driving?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I’m pretty careful with things like driving. Nothing like that comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Were you significantly disorganized or forgetful as a child, such as often losing things or forgetting assignments?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Back in grade school, teachers would tell my mother I was bright, but kind of scattered. I lost papers a lot, and didn’t always finish my work before I was… fidgeting or talking when I shouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following through on instructions or finishing chores at home?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t have a lot of chores like that now… I suppose I finish what needs doing, most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following through on tasks or activities in your leisure time?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. Like if I’m at a church planning meeting, or trying to read the bulletin, I’ll start off fine… and then after a bit I realize I missed a good part of what was said or written.\n\nDoctor: Did you exhibit excessive running, climbing, or physical restlessness as a child?\n\nPatient: Not so much running or climbing that I remember. More… restless, I guess. If I had to sit still too long, I’d get this feeling inside like I needed to get up, straighten something, or go check on something.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you lose track of time or forget about everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really. I might get interested in something for a while, but I can’t say I lose track of everything around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these symptoms at work or in other social situations beyond home and the community center?\n\nPatient: Well, I’m not working now, so I can’t really say about work. Socially… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed much beyond the usual places I go.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these symptoms when you're with friends or in other social situations outside of home and the community center?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t go out with friends much, and when I do, I haven’t noticed anything in particular.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’ve had little things here and there, but I can’t say it’s been consistent my whole adult life.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Even on quiet days, I’m bouncing between things—the garden club list, my knitting bag, travel papers—and I don’t really finish one before I’m on to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble completing tasks because you lose focus or get distracted?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Little things pull me off. If someone’s talking in another room, or a car passes the window, my mind just goes there, and then I’ve lost what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. If I’m sitting in a meeting, or just watching television, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot… or rubbing my fingers together. Sometimes I fuss with whatever’s in my lap without really meaning to.\n\nDoctor: Are these symptoms causing you a lot of problems or distress in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it is. It’s started to cause strain at home, and at the community center too. My husband and some of the volunteers… they’ve started checking behind me because I don’t always keep the plans straight. It’s embarrassing, really.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these symptoms when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t remember all of it clearly, but in grade school my teachers used to tell my mother I was bright, just… scattered. Losing papers, talking when I shouldn’t, wiggling around before I’d finished my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. My husband says I cut in before he’s finished talking. And in group planning, I’ll do it too… mostly because I’m worried I’ll lose the thought if I don’t say it right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before questions have been completed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… maybe once in a while, but I can’t say I’ve really noticed that as a regular thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty waiting for your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. Waiting in line at the pharmacy, or waiting my turn to speak at a meeting… I get fidgety. Sometimes I’ll catch myself sighing, or I jump in sooner than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often intrude on others physically, such as cutting in line or interrupting others' activities?\n\nPatient: Not physically, no… I don’t think I cut in line or anything like that. It’s more that I interrupt — my husband says I cut him off before he’s done, or in group planning I’ll jump in because I’m afraid I’ll forget what I was going to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do things impulsively, like buying things on a whim or making sudden decisions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any big way. Back in grade school, teachers would tell my mother I was bright, but kind of scattered. I lost papers a lot, and didn’t always finish my work before I was… fidgeting or talking when I shouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following through on instructions or finishing chores at home?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t have a lot of chores like that now… I suppose I finish what needs doing, most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following through on tasks or activities in your leisure time?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. More… restless, I guess. If I had to sit still too long, I’d get this feeling inside like I needed to get up, straighten something, or go check on something.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you lose track of time or forget about everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really.
